[?Agsterdan]
Greetings [?frem] [?Anctertcan,]
[?‘ais] evening finds Kay end me sitting [?quietly] [?‘tn] our little Pension, Hotel
[?DeMoor,] [?whils] [?tha] youngsters are out [?1.ti] Michele [?Dekking] at a4 party of
young people here in town, When we [?pn-ked] in just before leaving the .
senior [?Dckkings,] they all seemed to [?b-] having a good time (Dick talking).
Wednesday morning we were all [?u,»] bright and early, surprisingly well organ-
[?ized,] packed and ready to leave the house at 8:00 o'clock. The Porters
picked us up [?4nd] we headed for Metropolitan Airport in Detroit. We arrived
in [?plenvy] of time, registered, said farewell to the [?Porters,’] and went down
to board our American Airlines plane for New York City. Scheduled departure
was 11:00 o'clock; we finally left about 3:30 [?p.m.]
No particular details are necessary as it might have been many things, but
actually they couldn't get the motors started. It was a defective wire, but
they thought it was a starter, [?So,] after flying a plane in from Chicago with
a spare part, they finally found the defective wiring, corrected it, and we
took off, At this point we knew that unfortunately we were not [?golng] to be
able to spend much time with the [?Bamfords] in New York City. Our plans had
been to meet them and spend the afternoon chatting, playing a bit of bridge,
and perhaps letting the kids take a dip in the pool before heading for Europe.
Fate, however, deals in odd [?ways;] but we were just happy that the defective
wire developed on the ground instead of in the [?airi]
We [?£inally] arrived safely in New York without further incident, but only 45
minutes before departure time of our plane for Amsterdam. This meant a  
[?qreat] [?deal] of rushing in order to transfer from American to [?KLM,] although
[?J@] were met very gracefully by a [?KLM] representative and there was a bus to
take us over to their airport, [?"Bam"] met us at American, and we discovered
that they had had as frustrating.an afternoon as we had had, for all they
were aware of was the delay in departure time of our plane from Lansing,
and they had never received the wire we had sent [?them-=-they] probably got
that tonight! It was a jovial farewell that [?"Bam"] gave us, and we received
a wire from Ed ; as a [?bon] voyage, as well as two large suckers which I
am sure are the [?sam@] ones that we had sent to them just before they flew to
[?Burope.] Certainly a\six inch sucker [?ia] the last thing anybody needs as he
tries to board a plane [?ae] [?noe!]
At any rate, the [?KLM] transfer--although [?hurried--was] without particular
incident. We made it aboard the ship and had seats together, and the [?|]
flight took off on time at 7:30 p.m.
It was a beautiful flight. We did little besides eat and [?gip] on liquids
throughout the night, as any attempts at sleeping were most frustrating
in our rather cramped. quarters, Actually there were only about 2-1/2 hours
of sleeping time available to us, for our total flight was some 6-1/2 hours
including time for breakfast, Breakfast was a hearty meal considering that
we had just finished eating some three hours earlier. However, we managed
to eat most of it because we weren't sure exactly what would happen when we
arrived in Amsterdam. During the flight, our seatmate was a most pleasant:
young chap of 26 from [?Iran--one] Kim [?Dayani.] [?Nine] years ago he had [?landed]
[?in] New York unable to speak any English except to say, "I want to go to
New York." He had joined some friends in [?Misgouri,] and.that fall he started
to learn English. A few months later he entered college, and three years
later he entered medical school. He has just now finished his internship
at Denver, and is returning home for the first time, He added a bit of
life to our trip. [?|] [?fat] [?,]

[?‘Wa] [?axe] basically going to be trying to avoid the large cities and

Copenhagen, Denmark
July 1, 1966
Hie
We've had a busy time since our last letter to you, and will try
to bring you up to date. As I recall, we left off in Amsterdam
at the Ann Frank Houses and to give you a better description of
that, and also their own ideas regarding Amsterdam, Ann and Mary
will take their turns at the [?dictaphone,]
Ann speaking: Last Sunday we went to the Ann Frank House in
Amsterdam, which we found to be most interesting. The annex
which the Franks had was in the back of the building, where Mr.
Frank and Mr. Van Dan had their spice business. It consisted of
4 bedrooms, a kitchen, and an old restaurant. They moved into
this annex in 1942, and lived there for about two years until in
1944, when one German and 4 Dutch Nazi policemen suddenly stormed
upstairs and forced them to leave their annex. They were taken
to Auschwitz, the Nazi death camp in southern Poland. Everyone
in Ann's family, including herself, died in these concentration
camps except for Otto Frank, her father. He was freed in 1945,
[?Miet,] his former typist, had been in their annex since they had
gone, and recovered some papers of Ann's, He did not know at this
time that this was her diary. Without reading them, he gave them
to Mr. Frank and he read them. Had [?Miet] read them, he might not have
given them to him for reasons of safety. The diary is now written  
in many different languages, including German, and several million
copies have been sold. Thousands of people go through the Ann
Frank House each year, but “the Ann Frank House is not a monument,
not a memorial--it is a summons to speak out, to listen, and to be
willing to hear." Also on Sunday we went to the church, Our Lord
in the Attic. The name of this church comes from its being built
on about the fourth, fifth and sixth floors of a house, [?Several] of
these churches in the "attics" of houses were built just after the
Reformation, when the Catholics were driven from their churches and
compelled to worship in secret. Through time, all of the other
several hundred churches have been demolished, and now the Our Lord
in the Attic Church is the only one existing in Amsterdam. These
churches were not publicized, but those that did know about them
and were against them tolerated them. I particularly enjoyed the
architecture of it because it is older than any other church that I
have ever seen. I also found it very fascinating that they could
have the first three stories of a house look exactly like a house,
and then [?going] up into the next story you would go up a stairway
and all of a sudden you would be in a gigantic church. For this
[?peemen:] I found the church to be very unique, and enjoyed it all
the more,

[p. 15]
[?~-7] -

for all the farming land in the area that is behind the dykes that

water in the area, and some of this area could possibly be turned
into what they call [?“polters,"] which is land actually salvaged
from the ocean that they farm.on. About half of Holland--the
Netherlands--is now in the form of [?polters,] with more to be con-
[?structed] in the next 20 years. After viewing this project, you
can get a basic idea of what the Dutchmen have to go through to
Create land, and to protect the land that they have already created.
Since, as I have stated, about half of Holland is below the level of
the sea, quite a bit of caution has to be taken to protect this
land that they have below sea level, In 1953, they did have this
large flood in this area around the Rhine River, when many lives
were lost; and they are now trying to prevent this from happening
again. As we visited just as tourists, we really cannot feel the
total effect of this project; but it is certainly amazing to see
what they have done in merely 13 years since this flood, and what
they have planned to do in the next 10 years to dam off this whole
area. And just walking and viewing this whole project, you really
don't see how they could have done it, it's such an amazing project
and such a large undertaking, especially for such a small country
as Holland, really. This is due to be [?coppleted] around [?1930--the]
whole project including the [?dykes--although] they have the major
part of it completed now with these sluices completed. [?For] the

[?ested] me, and I find that they work somewhat like the swallows of

in the course of the evening they began to get into the feeling of the
good time and the friendliness of all. [?It] was a festival occasion with
a very few sideshow activities, but the center of attraction was a

[p. 45]
~5-
large tent in which about a [?204fece] brass band was playing dance music
of the German variety. I checked with one of the M. P.'s that was
wandering around, to find out what the general proceedings were. We
then walked in, found a table in the back and sat down, and very
shortly a young lady came along and we ordered our 2 beers and 3 cokes.
The music was, of course, terrific as far as Kay and I were concerned,
of the typical loud, brassy, fast Bavarian style. Shortly a young
German boy came over and sat down across the table from our two girls,
and began to chat with them; and we could make out from the general
hand motions that were going on that he wanted Mary to dance with
him--but in her shy manner she wanted to finish her coke first.
Soon two other chaps came along, one of whom could speak English,
and one of whom could not; so we had a terrific conversation between
the 5 of us, and after I bought them a round of beer the girls con-
descended to dance with them. I'm sure it was quite an experience
to add to their "Dear Diary" this evening, Actually it was a lot of
fun for all of us. We didn't stay as late as we might have because
we know that we have to get up early tomorrow morning. However, in
general I think it was just as well, because I didn't know where the
W. C. was out there arid was reaching the stage where the beer was
putting the pressure [?onl!]

all of this about [?2300] o'clock,
The weather was comfortable and dry, but cloudy; in fact, quite hazy,
And as we rolled into hilly country the distant [?hille] were lost in

The farming that we have seen to date is still most primitive, I was
quite surprised 20 years ago to find Germany [?80] mechanized in many
ways, but not so in farming. Such is still the situation in the area
that we have covered to date, as the fields are filled with people
(usually women) weeding, reaping, or using the scythe in what we
would consider a primitive manner. We, of course, have seen some
tractors and [?carta] and primitive reaping machines, but nothing com=-
pared to those that we have in our country. The moat startling thing,
of course, to the girls is to find the young girls and the women work~
[?ing] in the fields well into the evening, even on [?saturdays] and Sun-
[?days.] Perhaps this will make it a bit easier to get them to pull
some weeds in our own garden when we get home! However, I doubt it
because I think they will return to the American ways when we get
back into Lansing.
Before we left [?Rothenburg] today--in fact, as we were walking back
to our car and [?had] just stopped to pick up our bread, cheese and
[?wurst--we] could not pass a local [?beerhaus,] 80 we stopped outside
at a table for our two beers and three [?applesaft,] or apple juice.
We were there only a few minutes, but the small building was filled
with young people, and the waiter told us that these were all young
farmers (apparently the local 4-H group) that were in town for Sat~
[?urday] afternoon and evening, and apparently getting off to a good
start, This was actually the [?youngsters’] first experience at the
beer hall singing that we will encounter, I am sure, much more here
in Bavaria; and initially they [?wege] only lukewarm on the idea,
Tonight, however, they began to get into the general swing of things
a bit more,
Things are quieting down outside and beginning to quiet down a bit
in here, We are taking our turns at the shower down the line, and
will then turn in go that we can try to get up in good time tomorrow
to head into the Black Forest region.
Goodnight to all [?fromithe] [?"Pomeroys] [?funf."]
Monday Night, July [?11]
[?Tulingen,] [?Savaria]
I last talked with you in [?Crailsheim] two nights ago, Although we had
planned to be up early Sunday morning, we did not expect to be routed
out quite as soon as we were; for at 6:30 a brass band came marching
down the road [?directly] in front of our hotel. This was for the [?cele~]
[?bration] of the gymnasts' meet we had tumbled [?into;] but certainly a
German brass band beneath your hotel window at 6:30 in the morning
does not encourage sleeping in! As a result we were all up and down
for breakfast at shortly after 7:00. Actually there is a big differ-
[?ence] between trying to get 5 on the road and 2 on the road, [?howevery]
[?sO,] even though we were down to breakfast early, it was [?getill] [?8:30]
before we pulled out of town, We then followed [?tye] byways over to
[?Schwabiech-Hall,] farther west to the Autobahn, cutting west of [?stutt-]
[?gart,] and then left the Autobahn there, again hitting the back roads,
Then we cut through some 6f the byways southeast toward [?Kalmbach] and
then directly south through the Black Forest. The country north and
directly west of Stuttgart was comparatively flat, although a bit roll-
[?ing] up toward [?Schwabisch-Hall;] but as we moved down into the Black

[p. 48]
Forest area it became more beautiful and we could see how it was [?so]
well named, ‘This is a heavily forested area over an extensive period--
rolling hills, with but minimal scattered farming areas. Actually I
have not seen as many beautiful trees in Michigan, Pennsylvania, or
any place around the Middle West or East. Tall, beautiful [?conffers,]
[?gcattered] oaks, and basically a rather rocky, hilly area at the foot-
hills of the Alps. We worked down the eastern road of this [?Schwabischian]
area through [?Freudenstadt,] This is a rather attractive, but extremely
popular resort area without lakes, but with many nice hotels and [?appar-]
[?ently] many local spas. Although we had swung off from the main
[?Sehwarzwald] [?Strasse] because of traffic, we still found many people
out enjoying the holiday. The weather was beautiful--clear and sunny,
with a few scattered clouds. The people were certainly making the
best of this day, and I believe practically every nook and cranny was
filled with picnickers, or those that were out to spend the day and
enjoy the woods, Picnic tables or blankets were scattered along the
streams, along the hillsides, in almost any open area; and the [?fam-]
[?ilies] were making the best of it. Unfortunately we did not encounter
any of the old native dress--that is, the girls with the red [?pompons]
on their hats--although we went through many small towns actually
trying to see them, Perhaps, however, [?it] was too late in the day, as
it was afternoon by the time we were in this region and church was
out, From [?Freudenstadt] we cut west slightly, and then directly
south through [?Wolfach] to [?Triberg,] where we spent the night. We were
able to stop early about 4:30, wandered around the town a bit, did
some [?windowshopping,] and the girls found some souvenir shops in which
they picked up a few trinkets. The hotel where we stayed was about
the same as the previous hotels--rooms clean, large; the beds hard
(to our [?satisfaction};] with the wedge at the top which, of course,
we pulled out, and the extra-large pillows. We are beginning to get
used to the heavy feather comforters lying on top of us. Actually
they are not particularly heavy, and are not particularly [?uncomfort-]
able as far as warmth is concerned, Only rarely do I stick my feet
out at the end of the bed to cool them off; and they all seem to be
extremely sanitary, completely covered with a sheet and, of course,
fresh sheets on the bed itself. All of the hotels we have [?encoun-]
[?tered] have been extremely clean, The meals have been excellent,
although the toilet facilities are not up to American standards,
After dinner, Walt and I again wandered on the streets somewhat
while the girls returned to their diaries and Mother to some of her
note-writing, and then we all turned in comparatively early.
[?Triberg] is likewise one of the resort areas in the Black Hills,
although certainly not as well known as Baden Baden, I do not
believe there are any baths here; it is mainly the hills, ‘There is
a large pool, although we did not see it. It is beautiful country--
a beautiful falls which actually we did not see since they charged
[?a] [?mark] apiece in order to be able to look at it. We figured we had
seen many falls in the United States; and frankly about an hour [?pre=-]
[?viously] we had encountered a beautiful one as we were cutting through
the countryside. It seems strange to charge 25¢ to look at a tumbling
[?falis,] [?Triberg] is also somewhat of a winter resort, although of
course not as well known nor as popular as the Alps region.
This morning we were up and away in good shape--that is, considering
that there were 5 of us to get dressed, have breakfast, get packed,
etc.; and about 3:30 we pulled out of [?Triberg.]

In the course of the day at some time (I am not exactly sure when)
we crossed the equivalent of the Continental Divide, as the waters
that we were following yesterday and earlier this morning work their
way to the Rhine and thence to the Atlantic Ocean, ‘This afternoon, for
the [?first] time we encountered the Danube, but a small stream or river
that one could throw a stone across and barely navigable, We fol-
lowed this on into [?Tutlingen.] It was beautiful countryside, There
are some castles about § miles outside of town, but since it was
drizzling as we approached, we did not even try to go up to then,
and were anxious to get into town, wondering if we might be able to
visit the [?Jetter-sheerer] plant this afternoon, [?‘Tutlingen] is a fairly
industrial town, not large (I would guess possibly the size of Ann
Arbor or 4 little smaller), yet there are some 17 instrument [?manu-]
[?facturing] companies here in town. There are also some dozen [?instr-]
[?ment] designers and [?patternmakers,] who apparently work in a trade of
their own and are listed accordingly in the industrial book that we
locked at over at the hotel earlier this afternoon, This was [?some-]
what of a surprise to me, as [?I] did not [?realizé] there were so many
companies located in this one town,
[?put] I will have more to tell you about this [?tagmrrow] night, and I
certainly hope the weather fares better than today, because we
intend to head toward Zurich, [?Geodnight] for now, and our love and
best wishes to all of you back home from all of us.
Dick
Thursday, 14 July, 1966
[?Walenstadt,] [?switzerland]

We went over to the plant to meet Mr, [?Hinger] at 8:00 o'clock, and he

The big difference between [?Jetter-Sheerer] and any of their competitors
is the fact that they have their own drop forge plant, and get the raw
iron material and stainless steel from the Ruhr area and some from the

[p. 53]
[?Saar;] and by means of their own drop forge process and dies and their
own designs, they are apparently able to handle this business much more
economically than some of their competitors. They, of course, must have
the tool and die and the drop forge work done elsewhere. However,
basically the plant is, in my estimation, still some 15 ‘to 20 years
behind what one would anticipate. I had expected that a great deal
would all be hand-worked, and of course it is; but even in the drop
forge plant their mechanization principles are far behind ours in the
United States, I did not see a single conveyor in our tour through
the entire plant. Their only means of transportation between depart-
[?ments] is by hand cart. They have their own tool and die makers. we
moved on from the drop forge area and the stamping division, where
the rough instruments were produced, through the finishing, polishing
and final check area. There are at least seven checkpoints along the
line where defective instruments are picked up and discarded, and they
are practically all handmade, although by departments rather than by
one individual following through on a particular instrument. ‘The con-
[?struction] of the box lock and the way that this is made was most inter-
[?esting] to me, as I am very familiar with it in the use of our [?instru<]
[?ments.] Practically all of their instruments are stainless steel, but
they still use some brass and are still using some chrome plating for
those that have to be more malleable. [?Even] Kay and the girls found
this most interesting, and I am sure that the girls' version for
their "Dear Diary" is entirely different than [?walt's,]

Tuesday Evening, July 19
Still in [?Igls]

Today was a rather eventful day. We are all tired. We are back here
ac [?Haus] [?Kaserer] again this evening after taking off this morning and
planning on heading toward Munich. However, the unexpected [?happened--]
that fear which lies in the back of anyone's mind who is driving over
[?herew-that] is, the possibility of an accident. Fortunately, although
this could have been a serious accident, it was not too disastrous.
We were all very lucky. The car is in the garage being repaired, and
all of us are in the best of shape. Now as to what happened, and then
[?I‘ll] drop back to where I left off with our last letter.
This morning (Tuesday) [?werwere] up in fairly decent time, had our [?break-=]
fast, packed the car, and took off under cloudy skies ostensibly to
go north over the mountain range toward [?Oberammergau,] [?Garmisch,] and
[?ccward] Munich, hoping to be in or near Munich this evening. However,
as we were moving along the expressway outside of town (a dual high-
Way with fortunately not much traffic), we were approaching a cross
lane where we had the right-of-way when a lone bicyclist peddling
along on the right-hand side of the road suddenly and unexpectedly
turned directly across the highway in front of me to make a left-hand
turn onto the oncoming lane from the opposite direction. This, of
course, would have put him directly against traffic on the lane where
he was headed, and it carried him directly in front of us. The brakes
screeched as I swerved to the left to try and miss him, and we hit
the curb which was some 8 to 10" high in this area. We stopped rather
abruptly and then rolled over onto the right side of the bus. I recall
seeing the boy in front of the vehicle as we were rolling, but some of
the others were not sure whether he was in front of or alongside of us
as we rolled over. Fortunately almost all forward motion had stopped
at the time we rolled, although Walt (who was sitting in the right front
seat) slammed forward against the windshield with his head, shattering
it but not breaking it (the windshield, I mean), All of our luggage
was piled on the left-hand side or top side of the car; Mary was curled
up in the back seat [?trving] to rest; Kay and Ann were in the middle seat,
We wound up with the women on the bottom beneath some of the luggage,
boxes and miscellaneous things, including the bottles of Coca-Cola and
the few beers that we had in the car. I fell down on top of Walt, but
fortunately none of us were hurt. Walt reached around and slid the top
of the car back, and he and the womenfolk climbed out.onto the highway.
I opened the door on my side and climbed out the top side of the car.
All was certainly mixed up inside the car, and when the top was opened
so that everyone could climb out, many of the little things fell out
and scattered onto the highway [?alonside] of the car. The boy and his
[?bicycle] (he [?gusned] out to be a young French lad up here with a group)
was in front of our car. He had [?jumped] from his bike. The bicycle
was not hit and unhurt, and the boy himself was unhurt; but believe me
there were six scared characters around there for a few minutes! As I
was trying to gather my wits, a young [?chap.] [?stepped] up and spoke to me
in English to ask if he could be of any assistance, and he certainly
proved to be a Good Samaritan. I certainly avowed that he could help,

and he stayed with us until we were completely straightened around [?~—]

We [?ecade;] wandered and has over been to the with [?theatre] only rare where interruptions the Passion Play since is the produced pestilence every
years [?following] the Thirty Years’ War in [?1634.::I¢] [?was] at this time, as
you will [?recali,] that the townspeople of [?Oberammergau,] in order to [?avoid]

put on during the season, Much to our surprise, we learned that these
[?bast] all day long, starting at about [?8;30] in the morning and running

[?nen] who are going [?to.be] playing the lead parts must grow beards, and

[?snoes,] hats, helmets, spears, shields, etc., that were used in the play,

[?corium,] at $6.50, [?.]

and not be most desirous of first-class, plush circumstances all [?of»]

Friday, July 22, 1966
Dear Folks;
we are moving north of [?Fusen,] having just visited [?Neuschwanstein] [?and]
Viewed [?Hoenschwangau] Castle across the valley, and we are bouncing
along a blacktop highway as I dictate this. The day has been drizzly
and rainy; nevertheless at [?Neuschwanstein] we actually rode up the
[?ie] [?ial] and walked back down in the rain, with our raincoats and
[?wabxvellas,]
[?Neuschwanstein] is one of three castles built by Ludwig II, King of
[?Savarla.] It was started less than 100 years ago, but was still [?incom-]
[?plete] at the time of his death. He began its building at the age of
24 when he assumed the regency, but lived in it only at scattered
intervals over a period of approximately six months before his rather
mysterious death at the age of 41. The second castle on the [?Chiemsee,]
I saw in 1945, but we are not going to see it on this trip; and the
third [?one~-Linderhof--Kay] saw when she was over here in 1938, but we
[?wiil] not see on this trip. Both of these castles were actually com-
[?pleted] during the reconstruction period of [?Neuschwanstein.] ‘The king
also ha@ [?architects!] drawings and plans drawn up for a fourth castle
south of here, but this one was never started. They had constructed a
road up the mountainside, but no actual construction on the building
had begun at the time Parliament of this country declared him insane
and placed him under police guard near [?Muhich.] It was only a short
time after this that he and his physician went for a walk one day and
[?Cisappeared,] and subsequently both were found drowned in the lake.
Now let me turn the microphone over to one of the girls as we bounce
along this highway.
Ann speaking: In this large castle, the two rooms which impressed me
were the throne room and the king's bedroom. In King Ludwig's bedroom
were many hand-carved oak designs. The canopy of his bed resembles
Many church steeples and is all hand-carved oak. [?The] detail work
around his bedroom and on the ceiling is very beautiful. The guide
told us that it took 4-1/2 years for the sculptors to complete it,
with four men working on it. Just off his bedroom is a very small,
but pretty [?chasoel] that King Ludwig had built for himself. On the side
or this room there was a small running water faucet which was in the
shape of a swan. The running water came fresh from [?tne] mountain [?oppo-]
Site the castle. After it had run into the small bowl, there was a
[?smali] drain beneath it so the bowl could just be tipped over and the
water would drain out. In one of the corners of the room was a sitting
chair, where he sat to read. This chair was all hand-embroidered and
very beautiful, although the sun had faded some of its colors. There
were some beautiful swans embroidered on it, and swans were very [?promi-]
[?nent] through all of the castle in most of the decorations of the tapes-
tries and [?wood-carvings.] In the throne room, which consisted mostly of
marble, was a large, beautiful mosaic floor, which consisted of over
2,000,000 tiny pieces of marble and depicted several different kinds
of animals, Directly [?inthe] center of the ceiling was a huge gold
chandelier which was most impressive. This chandelier had candles in
it and weighed over a ton. The whole throne room resembled a church.
There were marble steps leading up to what you might imagine to be an

[p. 83]
altar, [?were] there should be a throne, but it was never made because the
King died [?before] the entire castle was completed. The throne was to be
[?an] elegant masterpiece of ivory and gold. On the ceiling in this room
there is detail work which is gold leaf. On the walls were portraits
of the 12 disciples, and above where the throne was to have been there
were six kings who were the past kings of Germany. ‘The kings who were
Favorites of King Ludwig were painted above the throne because they [?had_]
been canonized by the Pope. Above the six kings was the King of Kings,
Jesus Christ.
Dick speaking again, and I am breaking in merely to describe the country-
side as we move along. We have pulled out of the mountains and are in
rolling farm country as we move north toward Munich. Actually we have
just stopped at the [?Wiefkirche,] but will tell you about that later. We
are rolling through many small villages. The churches frequently have
white or pink walls, and onion-shaped domes rather than the sharp spires
[?predominant] in other areas, The people themselves in this area, of
course, are dressed in the typical Bavarian costume. Actually [?leder-]
[?hosen] are extremely common, and just a couple of minutes ago we passed
an old-timer who had a beard much like the well-pictured [?Maxmilian.]
we wore a little green hat with a feather, [?lederhosen,] and was load-
[?ing] his long, cherry-stemmed pipe. Earlier today as we were slowly
[?ascending] the hillside, barreling down on his bicycle came a chimney-
sweep, with his high stovepipe hat, and wire brushes bristling out on
all sides and curling around his neck. Walt commented that on all of
his trips through England he had not seen any. With his dirty face
and general [?apoearance,] he certainly reminded all of us of the [?chim-]
[?neysweep] in Mary Poppins, Farming is still rather primitive in this
area, We-see a great deal of scythe activity and some [?hand~-flailing.]
The hay is still stored in [?small] buildings scattered about the fields
for summer storage, [?rather] than in a large barn. The cows are the
tawny=brown such as we saw in Switzerland and Austria. I am quite
[?imoressed] by the general forestry projects [?here--the] upkeep of the
woods as they are extremely clean and neat; but of course this all
goes back to government-owned property. At the lake where we stayed
last evening (the [?Plansee)] this was all government-owned; and although
the lake is about five miles long and perhaps half a mile wide, there
are no cottages, no advertising and only two hotels--one at either end
of the lake--and three camping areas. The government will not allow
anyone to build a cottage or cabin on the shores of a lake, although
I know that if this were in our country it would be well surrounded by
[?private] homes. This, of course, is preservation of nature as it exists,
and it is along this line that our government is now trying to work,
but they are encountering difficulties as they attempt to purchase
private property and interfere with private enterprise and private
homes. We have stopped for the moment in a small town to take a pic-
[?ture] of what might be described as a Bavarian totem pole, although it
is not the type of carving that we encounter in our own totem poles,
it is a tall pole about twice the [?heighth] of a telephone pole, banded
in blue and white instead of the red and white barber pole paint.
Projecting off at various levels are carved designs (probably of metal)
of a dancing Bavarian in his [?lederhosen,] a young girl in her fluffy
petticoats, and various other signs and designs from a covered wagon
co a plow to 4 barn to a truck to a Christmas tree, none of which we
can interpret. Directly across from me is a small home and barn [?com-]
[?bined,] as is typical in this area, with a manure pile at the side and
a lovely flower and vegetable garden combined in front. Beautiful

Friday, 29 July, 1966
Luxembourg City
At breakfast this morning, which we had here in the hotel, we all
were reminiscing about our beautiful moonlight walk last night. The
lighting of the towers, the ramparts, the walls and the valley, with
the moon shining over the towers was most impressive. Actually it had
been a clear, cool, beautiful evening, and Alphonse knew the way to
lead us a rapid pace as we walked rather steadily for about an hour
over the [?cobblestoned] streets and hillsides of Luxembourg City.
Alphonse was at the front door to meet us this morning promptly at
[?93:00] o'clock. He directed us to a garage and was able to arrange it
so that they would give our car the 3,000-mile check, fix the horn,
etc., as well as wash it?s but we were going to be without the car until
5:00 [?o'clock] that night. Actually this 1s very good, because I think
under ordinary circumstances we would have been held up at least 48
hours for this activity. They seem to prefer plenty of warning over
here, and the Volkswagen garages are certainly busy.
We left the car and returned to the hotel to discuss our plans for
the day. The decision was that the youngsters and I would go out to
[?Hamm,] the national cemetery outside of Luxembourg, while Kay stayed
to do a bit of laundry and correspondence. This national cemetery is
most impressive with its white crosses row on row; and at the head of
all of them was a simple white cross--the grave of George Patton. The
memorial statues added a great sense of dignity and beauty to this
area, and it was a reminiscent few minutes that I spent there.
We returned the few miles to the hotel in Luxembourg City, and Alphonse
dropped us off for the afternoon so that we could wander around town.
We had a nice lunch at the [?Bahnhof,] and actually we are finding that
our noon meal is running heavier than usual lately, so I am sure we
are all going to gain weight over here. After lunch we took the
streetcar back to the heart of town, and walked in daylight around
part of the areas that we had covered the night before. We also went
down into the [?Cafemates,] which are the caves and tunnels built several
hundreds of years ago as part of the fortification area known as
the Gibraltar of the North. Returning to the hotel, Doctor and Mrs.
[?-Nies] and their daughter, Ann Marie, met us about 4:30. We drove out
to pick up our [?Volkswagen] and were held up a few minutes while they
were finishing this, then returned to pick up the girls at the hotel
and take off for the evening.
Off and on during the day the weather had been first cloudy, then
clear; but by [?evenihg] it settled down to a good, steady rain; and
although we had a beautiful evening ride, most of it was in the rain
and in an area that we would love to repeat in the daytime, since we
went on up to [?Vianden] and the area north of there along the Our River
[?ana] the German border. We all piled into our freshly-washed [?Volks-]
[?wagen;] it certainly looked nice and clean to take off in the rain.
We headed directly north through [?Mersch] over to [?Diekirch,] and then
up to [?Vianden.] One must remember that you can drive the full length
of Luxembourg in about two hours, and its breadth in about an hour
[?is] and a half; so actually although on the map it looks like a long
trip, [?timewise] it could be done very easily in a few [?hours’] time.
The unfortunate part was the weather, as it was raining while we
were going through some beautiful rolling, hilly, in fact somewhat
mountainous country. The castle at [?Vianden] is quite impressive. We

[p. 107]
[?ae]
looked at it through the light evening rain, but it was too dark to
try and take any pictures. Then we followed the road immediately
alongside the Our River, past their new dam north of [?Vianden] for
several miles, and cut back to the main highway at [?Hdofingen.] On this
main highway, we then cut south again toward [?Diekirch,] but left the
main highway there to cut diagonally over to [?Ettelbruck.] Of course,
all of this area was in the heart of the Bulge at the time of the war.
These [?ticular] names region. all rang [?Ettelbruck] bells, although is a city I which was not has involved recognized in the this Amer- par-
[?icans] to a great extent. They have an annual June Festival Day on
the anniversary of the death of Patton; they have a memorial to Patton
and have named a street after him; and their main street is Rue [?de]
J. F. Kennedy. We then cut back through a beautiful little valley,
still in the rain, to the little off-the-beaten-track town of [?Weischeid]
and went to one of the local inns. Since it was after [?93:00] when we
arrived, they were not too anxious to feed us, but they did give us a
Sample of their unique and delicious ham. This is a delightful little
inn in a very small town of probably Only a couple of hundred people,
yet surprisingly this inn and restaurant are well-known. People from
the Gymnasium in [?Ettelbruck,] and-also from Luxembourg, which is about
an hour away, frequently come out here for dinner apparently. We had
a very nice meal, and the beer certainly tasted good after this drive
which was made longer by the weather. Then we headed back on the main
route to Luxembourg City. Alphonse finally dropped us off at our hotel
a little after 11:00, and we were all rather tired and ready to turn in
for the night. [?;]
[?Bon] [?nuit.]
Saturday, July 30, 1966
This has been another of Dad's days, although I think everybody has
enjoyed it as we wandered around through [?Larochette] and the areas where
I spent memorable days in December of 1944,
Alphonse met us again at 9;00 o'clock this morning, though his wife and
daughter remained at home; so Walt drove the Volkswagen with the girls
while I rode with Alphonse as we headed toward [?Larochette.] Alphonse
had an afternoon lecture to give at the school, so he was going to have
to return at 3:00 o'clock, and we were going on to [?Bastogne] by our-
selves, We went first to Echternach--a town which was badly hit [?dur-]
[?ing] the war, and nothing but a few ruins the last time I saw it: but
it is now well rebuilt, and with a center plaza that is basically a
tourist reception area. The old church has been completely rebuilt
and they were in the process of having a wedding as we arrived, which
added a little novelty to our experience there and removed part of the
stigma Of the tourist stores in this area.
We left [?Echternach] and returned to [?Scheidgen,] where I readily spotted
the old creamery which we had been in for several days during the [?pre-]
Bulge period, and through a few other towns that brought memories, such
as [?Consdorf,] and then over to [?Larochette.]
We stopped at Alphonse's home, where I had spent several days during
the Bulge with him and his parents, and where I first met him. His
mother died some two years ago, but his father is still living; and
although he knows practically no English he was most gracious. We

[p. 108]
joined him in a wine and a grape juice for the youngsters for a few
minutes, as we chatted about the few things that we could converse

Tuesday Morning, August 2, 1966
Paris
The Last two days have been busy and it has been late at night when we
have turned in, so I haven't had a chance to bring you up to date on
Our activities.
[?Somahow] we managed to leave Luxembourg City early Sunday morning,
Alphonse again met us a few minutes before 9:00. We had the car packed.
and were en route out of town by 9:15. This is somewhat of a record
for our family of five. Alphonse drove us to the edge of town where
we bade him a fond farewell, hoping to meet again in the United States,
as he may be coming with his family to spend some time; or his daughter
may be coming to the States when she is a bit older than her present
14 years.
We drove west out of Luxembourg directly toward [?Rheims,] as we were all
anxious to see the cathedral there. It was rather slow driving along
the back roads, although there was very little traffic even though it
was Sunday. However, the winding French roads are not as well repaired
as either the German or the Luxembourg secondary roads. It was a nice
day, sunny but cool, and we arrived at [?Rheims] at about 2:30 in the
afternoon. ‘
Our weather, incidentally, is apparently [?somdwhat] of a contrast to
that at home, according to the few reports we have had. All that we
hear from the States is how hot and dry it has been, and we certainly
wish we could exchange some of our wet, drizzly weather or cloudy
weather with you. I am sure it would be of more help to you, and
some of your sun would be of help to us. The days have been generally
cool, [?and.] [?we] have been more than satisfied to wear our jackets or
coats; and I think we have had more than our share of cloudy and
[?vainy] days for a family who is on vacation. Actually it is cloudy
again today, and this morning after sleeping in we are relaxing and
doing laundry here on the outskirts of Paris while we are trying to
catch up on some [?corréspondence,] and then we will be going down into
Paris this afternoon.
But back to [?Rheims.] We drove into town and down to the cathedral,
which is certainly most impressive. It is a beautiful [?latticwork,]
twin-towered structure which is familiar to all of you, and we were
fortunate enough to [?encounter:an] English-speaking guide who was most
enthusiastic in describing the cathedral and giving us its history.
We spent an hour wandering around with him in and out of the various
nooks and crannies, as he explained the history of this church, which
goes back to Clovis--the first Christian king of France. All of the
kings of France were crowned in this cathedral. Napoleon was the
first [?DBmoeror] who was not, as he was crowned (or actually crowned
himself) in [?Notre] Dame Cathedral of Paris. This little jaunt
delayed us so that we were late getting into Paris--something I had
hoped to avoid--and about all I can say is that we have proved a [?fam-]
[?ily] of five can come into Paris at 9:00 o'clock at night without [?reser-]
[?vations] and get accommodations. After studying the map and the sub-
way, Or Metro, plans, we decided to stay in [?Vincennes] on the outskirts.
We drove in here about 8:30, to find the facilities are basically

that was half-way satisfactory. We are set up again at the moment

with two doubles and.a single, each with either a shower or a bath of

After our Continental breakfast yesterday morning we went across the

Yesterday morning we took what they call the tour of modern Paris,
going through the heart of modern [?Paris--Place] [?de] la Concorde, seeing
the Obelisk, the Palace, over to the Invalides--originally built as a

other well-known French statesmen or soldiers. A guide took us

really did a beautiful job. We then moved on through the Arc [?de]
[?Triumphe] up into the [?Montmartre] area and the Church of Sacre-Coeur.

into the church itself. This is a beautiful edifice of mixed [?archi-]
[?tecture,] yet quite impressive. The view of Paris, of course, is beau-

take a picture in the sunshine, but it takes a little bit of practice
to be able to take any kind of picture in the [?rainl]
Paris is in a clean-up area. They have set a goal of seven years in
which to have all of their buildings scrubbed, so many of the old black
buildings are now turning white. Basically they are scrubbing them
with water only, because the stone is so old that if they used too
much soap or detergent this would destroy the stones themselves. We
returned to the American Express, then had lunch at [?Pam=-Pam] down the

Friday, 5 August, 1966
[?Amboise,] France

Hello, again.
A few little passing thoughts I'd like to share with you before I start
in to describe today's escapades. As we sat around the dinner table this
evening we were reminiscing about some of the events of the past few days,
many of them little things that had impressed us at the time. First, I
think I mentioned the delightful chap who took us around Versailles and
passed on many little pearls of wisdom. We asked him [?abou] the toilet
facilities at the time of Louis XIV and found that it was customary in
those days to have a box in the corner--fleur-de-lis and all--for the
use of his royal bottom; and that it was actually considered quite an
honor to be the aide who had the job of emptying this. My, how times
have changed! The young chap also commented that each morning it took 60
people to dress our friend, Louie. Each was well designated as to his
activities: one for the right foot, one for the left foot, one for the
right glove, one for the left glove, one for the cane and, of course, one
for each little item of clothing along the line. Lou became bald at an
early age, hence his many wigs; thus he was well pleased with the [?popu-]
[?larity] of wigs in his day. Since he needed a wig and could well afford
it, he had a great many of them and seemed to change them approximately
every hour. He would go through 15 or more wigs in a day, and had a
variety of colors, Our guide friend also had a bit of a sense of humor.
At Versailles, one point he when referred he was to explaining Charles [?de] a particularly [?Gaulle] as the glamorous "second Louis area [?xIv,] in "
[?ying] this with a chuckle and a sly glance around the room. Like most
Frenchmen, he was most conscientious and helpful to Kay, repeatedly
calling to "Madame" to make sure that Kay understood what was going on

After leaving the castle in the fog and haze, we drove past the

Hangman's Corner. The gatekeeper had also told us that several [?sKél-]
[?etons] were encountered when they put a new road through this region
a few years ago. We then drove back to the little village of Berry-
[?Pomeroy,] which has approximately the same population that it had at
the time of the Doomsday Records--about 200 or 250 people. We visited
the little church, and found it doubly interesting in that it had been
originally constructed by one Richard [?Pomeroy] in the [?15th] Century,
\ around and over a small pre-existing Norman church. Subsequently it
was, and still is, used as the burial place for many of the [?Seymours,]

[p. 135]
Within this very small church, off from the Sanctuary on the north
side lies a rather impressive sarcophagus of the [?Seymours.] On the
same side, but in a [?different] area are the plagues and the tombs of
the [?Pomeroys.] Outside, the graveyard on the south side of the church
is filled with old crosses and tombstones, most of which are illegible.
There is also a beautiful yew tree which had been planted in 1792,
[?ead] an even more beautiful tree of the type that could supply many
[?Christmas] wreaths,
ft was almost 6:00 o'clock when we left the church, but as we moved
[?€o] the outskirts of town we passed a field where they were cleaning
up after a sheep sale; so, even though it was late, we drove in and
watched them drive the remaining ewes onto trucks to take them back
either? to the farms or, I suspect, possibly a number of them to
[?slaughterhouses.] We then returned to our little cottage in [?Dartington,]
The Dawn," and went over to the local inn for dinner. It was [?delight-—.]
[?ful,] full of atmosphere, and the kidney and steak pie that I had was
certainly out of this world. It was still drizzling as we returned
home, and since we had not covered quite as much ground in this area
as we had hoped to, we decided definitely to stay over for another day.
We were up this morning in time for our 8:30 breakfast. Again we
found [?the] weather misty and hazy outside, and certainly anything but
a good day for traveling. However, we returned to [?Totnes.] [?walt] and
I finished our wanderings around through the old gates, along the old
wall, into the old guild house and church, while the girls were shop-
ping and Kay was getting her hair "frizzed." We all met at noon and
headed down along the Dart River to Stoke Gabriel, where we stopped at
the Castle Inn for a light lunch of sandwiches and drinks. From there
[?'] we drove over to [?Paignton.] Here we located a wash and [?ary] laundry and
gave our clothes the first good washing they have had in some time.
Frankly I think they were getting tired of being washed out in our
[?itocal] sinks. In Holland, Germany or Austria, however, we had not used
the laundry method because laundry had to be left for a day or so in
order to get the clothes washed and dried; it was not just a matter of
dropping in, running them through the washer and dryer and completing
the project in a short while. This we were now able to do, and while
our clothes were washing we drove along the coast a few miles up to
[?Torquay] to see the very small, crowded beaches in this area. We
decided certainly this was no place that we would care for as a summer
vacation or holiday, as hundreds and thousands are doing at this time.
We returned, picked up our laundry, and then headed south for Dart-
mouth, crossed the river by [?sidewheel] ferry at [?Kingswear,] and drove
out to the old fort and castle at the mouth of the river. There we had
tea at 4:00 o'clock, and then cut diagonally across country and back
into [?Totnes.]
AS we approached the ferry, the sun began to break through, and the
rest of the afternoon and evening has been beautiful. The weather
report is for good weather tomorrow, both on our radio and on the T. [?Vv.]
which Walt watched downstairs,
Cutting across country we again took to many of the smaller roads. ‘The
hedgerows and bushes were twice as high as the car, so actually it was
only on occasion that we were able to see any of the rolling countryside.
[?However,] it was beautiful, and although the roads were narrow we were

[p. 136]

fortunate and did not encounter very much [?traffic,] I don't think even

When we returned to [?Totnes] the sun was still out, so we cut back through

[?ings.] It is a place where one could wander around for many hours

pack at "The Dawn" in good time, washed up, and this evening went over

pointed arches, dates from 1413 and is one of Devon's oldest bridges.
It was certainly an attractive and unusually shaped bridge, and I hope
our pictures turn out.
We are back at "The Dawn" now. Kay has just finished a bit of iron-
[?ing,] and the tub is in use as we are all bathing and getting ready for
an early departure tomorrow. We plan to head north toward [?Exeter]

Among the knights who joined with William at this time was one Ralph
Ge La [?Pommeraye,] and apparently a brother of his also, although as
yet I have been unable to learn very much about the latter. [?Appar-]
[?ee] [?ently] Ralph was the boy of the [?hour--a] Baron and land-holder, and from
what I have been able to learn, quite an Army officer as well. I do
not know what happened to the brother, as very little is mentioned
about him; but I assume he did not remain in England very long fol-
lowing the invasion, and probably returned to the Caen-Bayeux area.
Ralph, however, did right well by himself, and as a reward for his
services received not only the land and the village around what is
now called Berry-Pomeroy, but 56 other similar areas in Devon and
two [?Somersetshire] manors. This ranked Ralph as the fourth or fifth
largest baronial landholder in [?Devonshire,] which includes the whole
southwestern part of England. Those rated above him included the
Sheriff, who at that time held a much greater political status than
at the present time; one [?Juhel,] who obtained the property around
[?Totnes] and was responsible for the construction of the castle which
is in [?Totnes] itself; and the ecclesiastical property owners. [?Appar-=]
[?ently] most of the other areas were smaller and not as pleasant as
the area around Berry-Pomeroy, so Ralph decided to build his castle
here. A previous defensive structure had been built here by the
[?Saxons,] and it was around this area that Ralph constructed the walls
and the original castle of La [?Pommeraye.]
When I first saw Berry~Pomeroy Castle, I was struck by its [?similarity:]
both in location and in general outline, as near as I could tell, to
La [?Pommeraye] in France. It is built on the edge of a precipitous
[?a] hill with a single approach across a moat. It is not absolutely [?cer-]
[?tain] whether this was a wet or dry moat, although apparently it was
wet as a stream runs nearby. There is a magnificent view of the
valleys in the distance from this hilltop. Ralph must have been a
rather rugged character, and he was most loyal to William. A prison
was established a few miles away, and poachers or those who hunted
or otherwise broke the baronial statues were tried by Ralph in the
castle, then removed to the prison and subsequently hung on what
became known as Hangman's Corner. Taxes were collected in the local
village; Ralph took his cut, the Sheriff got his cut and, of course,
William got his share.
the name, Berry, actually apparently comes from [?"Beri,"] referring to
a home or castle, and is not a family name. In other words, [?Berry=]
[?Pomeroy] refers to the home of the [?Pomeroys.]
Ralph died in 1102, although the exact date of his birth is unknown.
His oldest son, William died two [?years..later,] Apparently he was not
quite the man that Ralph had been, and he gave the property to some
of the monks of Gloucester. After his death it was his brother,
[?Gosselin,] who fell into line; and apparently he appeased the monks by
establishing the Abbey of [?Notre] Dame Du Val back in Bayeux, France.
By so doing, he was able to regain the [?Pomeroy] estates in the area of
[?Berry-pomeroy,] SO actually there was only a two-years' period that
the land was temporarily out of [?Pomeroy] hands.

[p. 141]
At any rate there was an exchange of funds, and even though the siege
was successful, apparently some funds were paid to the [?Pomeroy] family
by Lord Somerset when the castle was taken over by the Seymour family
in about 1548. The Lord's son by his first wife, Sir Edward Seymour,
was apparently the first Seymour to move into the castle and start
making alterations,
OF course, anyone moving into someone [?else's] old mansion is never sat-
[?isiied] with it, just as any First Lady moving into the White House
encounters the problem of changing the rooms to her own satisfaction.
Such was the [?case] with the [?Seymours;] so they began to tear down some
of the old areas and build it up in the Tudor fashion into a new
enclosure, ending up with a tremendous mansion or housing facility of
an entirely different character. This is still visible today, with
its many large, rectangular windows, the tremendous ballroom and the
multiple [?fireolaces,] and probably for a short period of time the [?struc-]
[?ture] was a beautiful mansion rather than a fortified castle,
The [?Seymours] continued to live in the castle for a short while, although
it was under construction and never actually completed during their
occupancy. Then more political problems ensued, [?and] “it seems to be
a fair guess that, had not the second Baronet and [?his.son=-another]
[?sdward;] born in 1610 in Berry, and representative of [?Devonshire] in
[?London-~joined,] to their financial undoing, the Royalist side in the

[?mours,] incidentally (the first Edward) was an.Ambassador to Denmark [?in]
[?the] early 1600's, Subsequent [?Zdward] [?Seymours] (father and son) were in
fairly good political standing for a time in the [?mid-17th] Century, but
‘then encountered problems. The senior retained a Governorship of Dart-
[?nouth=—aoparently] a rather remunerative position: but in 1645 they both
fell into Parliamentarian hands. The senior (now well over 70) was
sent to London as a prisoner, but was subsequently exchanged, while
the junior (aged 36) was retained in prison, The senior [?died] in 1659
[?anc] is buried in the Parish of Berry-Pomeroy. The son-~apparently a
better politician, talked his way out of his problems, and in 1660 he
was back in the Berry again and re-entered Parliament for [?Devonshire,]
where he sat for [?Totnes] in all four Parliaments between 1661 and 1687.
He lived long enough to welcome William, Prince of Orange, to Berry in
November [?Of] 1688 and to receive, the Governorship of [?Exeter,] but he died
within a month and was buried at Berry-Pomeroy.
Regarding the subsequent history of the two families, apparently the
[?Pomeroys] split up after the [?Seymours] took over the castle, some of
the moving to America for religious protection, [?and] other remaining
in England, [?Eltweed] [?Pomeroy,] of [?Beaminster] in Dorset, sailed with

[?Found] [?Dorchester,] [?Massachusetts,]
The fourth Seymour, as mentioned before, left the Berry [?some] time
between 1683 and 1701, and made his home at Maiden Bradley. There he
died. [?Seymours] subsequently have retained their Dukedom, and although
some three [?centuries] have passed since a Seymour occupied the castle,

[?jonally,] and there is talk that is may soon be taken over as a national

Wood [?Henge.] This is a much smaller structure than Stone [?Henge.] There
is very little left of the original because it had been built of wood
that has long since disappeared. Again this dates back a couple of
thousand years B. C. Actually the general area around the plains of
Salisbury is studded in many regions with the ceremonial rocks and
pillars of prehistoric man. We read with interest the description of
[?Azebury,] and the comment that it was to Stone [?Henge] what a cathedral

We turned east of Marlborough to the small town of West [?Kennett,] and
then up to [?Azebury.] The town is actually in the middle of the [?Azebury]
circle of stones, I believe all of us were more impressed with this
than we had been with Stone [?Henge,] and came away with a bit more of
belief, if not of understanding, in what had taken place at a time
as Many years [?B.] C. as our present time in the [?20th] Century. In fact,
[?Azebury] seems to date back about 4,000 years B. C. It covers a much
larger area than Stone [?Henge,] but the stones are smaller (although the
heaviest weighs over 40 tons). There are about 100 of them still
remaining, and they form a series of concentric circles in the center
of which lies the small community of [?Azebury] itself. There are four
roads leading into this community, making four entrances through the
concentric ring, with a deep ditch and earthwork ridge surrounding
it. These are probably the same four entrances that were used orig-
[?inally.] The largest stones were used as portals, and they were found
near the roads that exist currently. In the Middle Ages these stones
were systematically tipped and buried; but during the [?18th] Century when
the ground was cleared for agricultural use they were uncovered and
many were broken up and used as building materials. As archaeological
interest developed, the remaining ones were saved and reverted to their
Original upright positions. A great deal of excavation activity was
carried out here immediately before the war, and some has been done
since. There is also a very interesting museum nearby, containing
many relics which had been removed during the archaeological surveys,
not only from [?Azebury,] but also from other surrounding Stone Age and
Bronze Age areas that have been uncovered in this region. The sun [?con~]
[?tinued] to shine and it was quite warm, so we had an ice cream instead
of our afternoon tea before we left town, then headed north into the
Cotswold region. Certainly anybody who is interested in the old [?his-]
[?tory] of this area at all would find the short visit to [?Azebury] much more

[?ning] to leave our car here in London and fly to Dublin on Thursday.

traffic was at a minimum, Walt has done a beautiful job, and we are
all safe and sound.
Goodnight from London
Dick

Monday, 22 August, 1966
London, England
This morning was one of more or less individual action; at least, [?walt]
and I went in one direction and the girls went in another. Breakfast
here in England is included with the room charges, and is more like
the type of breakfast we are used to at home. This morning we started
out with juice, followed by cereal if anyone wished, and then bacon or
sausage and eggs prepared according to individual choice, plus coffee
Or tea. So after breakfast Walt and I went to the American Express
office intending to mail a package, only to learn that it was too small
to comply with the regulations; so we cashed some travelers' [?cheques,]
[?wandered] around a bit and checked on shipping the car home, then returned
to the hotel about 12:30 to meet the girls.
we went around the corner to the local Wimpy for lunch, where the young-
[?sters] had a hamburger, Kay had a salad and I had an omelet, then we took
the car and drove around London for a bit. Actually the traffic here is
not as bad as it was in Berlin or Munich, and certainly not nearly as
bad as in Amsterdam; and I wouldn't even attempt driving in Paris. We
wanted to see the old Curiosity Shop so we drove over, but we hit there
just at the peak of the bus tours, and about ten seconds inside was long
enough for me. I went outside and enjoyed looking at this modern tourist
shop from there. [?Immediately] around the corner was the Royal College of
surgeons, so I stopped there hoping to be able to visit the [?Huntarian]
Museum, but found that it is closed during the month of August. How-
ever,.they were kind enough to allow me to go up the stairs and peek
through the windows at some of the skeletons and anatomical dissections
which Hunter had done, and to see their collection of old surgical [?7]
instruments. Walt and Ann had come along with me, and seemed most
interested as I told them what little I knew about the place.
From there, we went over to St. Paul's Cathedral where we spent two
enjoyable hours. This, of course, is a beautiful Wren [?masterpiece.]
Stained glass windows do not play a major part in its architecture;
rather, the high nave and the tremendous dome are its outstanding
features. Kay and I went as far as the outside stone rim in our climb-
[?ing,] and the youngsters went a bit higher; but we all got a good view
of London from this area. The Whispering Gallery is most unusual and
interesting, Actually this was not really recognized until the [?struc-]
[?ture] had been completed. We continued to wander around a bit and had
a cold drink, for we were tired and thirsty by this time. [?Weatherwise,]
we have had no rain today, [?but'it] has been humid and cloudy.
After we left the museum we went to the other extreme and visited
Madame [?Toussaud's] Wax Museum. We spent a [?littlé] over an hour wander-
[?ing] around there, seeing all of [?the] [?characters] of [?antient] and modern
history depicted in her wax [?effigies,] and enjoyed this tremendously.
I had not realized before that this museum actually originated during
the French Revolution, and while Madame [?Toussaud] was in prison under
guard, she was forced to make the plaster and.then the wax replicas
of the famous people of that time, including the representation of [?©]
Henry XVI and Marie Antoinette, whose heads were brought to her [?ina]
box! Although she was suspected to be a Royalist supporter, they
recognized her as an artist, [?’]
We returned to the hotel and cleaned up a bit, [?Eben] [?We] [?nce] [?coe] [?iee] [?mn]
for dinner, and afterward Kay and the youngsters went [?toa] [?nee]

[p. 165]
("How to Steal a Million"). I certainly hope they learn the secret
which would help to finance this venture, at least [?inspart.] [?However,]
I elected to remain here at the hotel and read and catch up On some
[?';)Odds] and ends, and to relax in this manner rather than in a movie theater.
Tomorrow we will have our last venture in the car. We plan to visit
Hampton Court, Windsor Palace and, we hope, the [?Kew] Gardens; then Wed-
[?nesday] morning we will turn the car over for Shipment back to the States.
London is another city that has changed its face completely since I last
Saw it in 1944, They have done a beautiful job of replacement, but the
new white buildings are rather startling in contrast to the old ones.
St. Paul's, of course, has been well restored, including the beautiful
new chapel at the east [?end:] [?of] the choir--the area which was rather com-
[?pletely] destroyed by the bombings of 1940, It is amazing that almost
the entire encircling area was bombed out, whereas the basic structure
of St. Paul's with its dome remained [?unscathed.] Actually only two or
three bombs hit the cathedral itself, and considering the extent of the
bombings at that time, the total amount of damage done was not great.
Speaking of churches, I am not sure I mentioned the little st, Augustine
Church in [?Brookland,] in the Romney marsh area which we passed through [?a]
few days ago. This is not like writing a diary, where one can look back,
SO occasionally I am not sure what I may have missed. At any rate, to me
this little church was one of the most unusual that we have seen this
summer. It was rare not because of its age, since it dates back only
to about the [?15th] Century; but its first strange feature is that the
steeple itself is not atop the church, but on the ground beside it.
This steeple, or bell tower, is an Octagonal wooden structure harboring
five bells, and sits on the ground just north of the church. There are
apparently numerous stories as to why this is so, but I think the most
Obvious one is that actually the church itself was built on marshy
ground and could not withstand the added weight of the tower, [?‘The]
local people are not sure whether the steeple was ever On top of the
church, but one story has it that it was placed up there and fell off
twice, and that eventually the church members gave up trying to replace
it and just placed it alongside the church structure. At any rate, it
now sits on the ground, but is still tall enough to be seen from the
flat countryside surrounding the area. The church itself is gradually
settling into the marsh, although they claim it has sunk Only about half
an inch in the past 70 years. The pillars inside are spreading apart
due to the weight of the roof and the softness of the ground underneath,
giving an unusual appearance. Part of the south arcade apparently leans
out about one foot in its overall height of about 14 feet, [?and] this is
Supposed to be theoretically beyond the point of collapse. it is [?cer-]
[?tainly] a strange sight to step into this small church and see the [?pil-]
[?lars] of the nave leaning apart. Flying buttresses have been applied in
an attempt to hold these pillars in place, and they hope they have thus
controlled the spreading. Another of the main points of interest in this
church is the circular leaden baptismal font, which is apparently one of
30 remaining in this country and has most beautiful workmanship. [?Appar-]
[?ently] it came from Normandy many years ago, but exactly when or how is
uncertain. Undoubtedly during some of the raids many centuries ago, it
was brought home by some of the seamen in this coastal area. It must
[?de] recalled that Romney was one of the important [?cinque] ports of the
[?entish] coast; although nowadays it is inland, surrounded by grazing
land, and a quiet farming community. The seafaring days, the "rum-
running" days, and the raiding days into Normandy are long gone.

[p. 166]
Part of our crew has returned; Kay and the girls just got in, and
apparently enjoyed their movie. Walt elected to walk back alone so
[?’] that director" he could in this see London area) will by night. be up and. I imagine around that and raring he (as to our go.in "tour the
morning, as we head west of town; so I'll close for now and try to
pick up the thread of our story again tomorrow night.
Dick
Tuesday, 23 August, 1966
Today has been lovely. It was a bit cloudy this morning, but cleared
off this afternoon, with only.a few scattered clouds visible.
We did not get away quite as early this morning as we might have liked;
but as I think I have commented before, with five of us someone is
always a little bit late--sometimes the girls, sometimes [?walt,] and
sometimes Mother or me--usually it is Mother or me! This morning it
happened to be the girls who delayed us: but at any rate we finally got
under way and headed toward Windsor Castle. I functioned as navigator
with a map on my lap, while Walt weaved his way through traffic on the
wrong side of the road and we got through town, and within an hour we
were in the town of Windsor. Walt had been here last year, and when
he got within gunshot of the place he knew where he was headed, so he
drove into a parking lot and we walked back to the castle. We walked
through the [?gateand] into the cathedral. This is a beautiful edifice;
it is a bit more square [?ceilinged] than some of the others we have seen,
but is filled with the customary tombs of royalty. Many of these are
of rather recent origin. We wandered through the courtyard and into
the royal chambers, and here we used the tape recorder to good advantage.
I would suggest that anyone taking a tour of this type could put a tape [?_.]
recorder to good use. [?Hach] of us wandered around with our little ear
phones and also with [?a] [?recorder;] thus we could wander from room to room
and pick up the descriptions without bothering others who wished to roam
through in a more rapid manner. This took about 45 minutes, but was
highly entertaining and most informative. Walt had our tape recorder,
and was working with a double [?earphone’] [?set-up.] All went well [?unti] he
reached the last room. At that time his tape ran out and got a bit
snarled, and he was in the process of changing it when one of the guards
came up and questioned him. However, he soon got the tape recorder adjusted
properly and they allowed him to continue. At any rate, we have a fair
recording of the description.of the castle for the youngsters to listen
back to when they get home, or for anyone else to listen to who wishes
to do so,
